They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   119→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   120→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   121→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   122→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   123→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   124→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   125→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   126→\n   127→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   128→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   129→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   130→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   131→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   132→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   133→\n   134→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   135→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   136→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   137→\n   138→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   139→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   140→\n   141→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   142→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   143→\n   144→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   145→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   146→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   147→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   148→\n   149→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   150→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   151→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   152→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   153→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   154→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   155→\n   156→## Python environment\n   157→\n   158→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   159→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   160→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   161→\n   162→## Decoder Reference\n   163→\n   164→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   165→- Optional arguments:\n   166→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   167→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   168→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   169→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   170→\n   171→### Purpose\n   172→\n   173→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   174→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   175→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   176→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   177→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   178→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   179→\n   180→### Consistency requirements\n   181→\n   182→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n   183→- Loading of data\n   184→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   185→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   186→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   187→\n   188→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   189→\n   190→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   191→\n   192→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n   193→\n   194→Carefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n   195→\n   196→You will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n   197→\n   198→---\n   199→\n   200→## Conversion Workflow\n   201→\n   202→**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Each step has explicit completion criteria. Do not proceed to the next step until the current step is complete. Use the exact step names below when documenting progress in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   203→\n   204→**STATUS TRACKING**: At the start of each step, update its status to `IN PROGRESS` in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. When complete, update to `COMPLETE`. This helps you and the user track progress.\n   205→\n   206→---\n   207→\n   208→### Step 0: Setup and Initialization\n   209→\n   210→**Goal**: Set up the working environment and create the notes file.\n   211→\n   212→**Actions**:\n   213→1. **FIRST**: Create `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` with the template structure shown at the end of this document\n   214→2. Verify you can run `python3` and import key packages (numpy, torch)\n   215→3. List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\n   216→4. **REMINDER**: Do NOT look at any files outside this directory\n   217→\n   218→**Done when**: \n   219→- `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\n   220→- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\n   221→- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\n   222→\n   223→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\n   224→\n   225→---\n   226→\n   227→### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\n   228→\n   229→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   230→\n   231→**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\n   232→\n   233→**Actions**:\n   234→- Look for documentation or README files in the `code` directory\n   235→- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference paper loaded and manipulated the data\n   236→- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\n   237→- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \n   238→- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \n   239→- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\n   240→\n   241→**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 1\".\n   242→\n   243→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   244→\n   245→---\n   246→\n   247→### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n   248→\n   249→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `data` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   250→\n   251→**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\n   252→\n   253→**Actions**:\n   254→- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\n   255→- Determine available variables and their meanings\n   256→- Check data dimensions and data types\n   257→- Look for documentation or README files\n   258→- Note the data organization and structure\n   259→- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\n   260→\n   261→**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 2\".\n   262→\n   263→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   264→\n   265→---\n   266→\n   267→### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n   268→\n   269→**Goal**: Extract key information from the paper and methods.\n   270→\n   271→**Actions**:\n   272→- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\n   273→- Look for and note to file:\n   274→  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\n   275→  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\n   276→  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\n   277→  - Variables important to experiments\n   278→  - Temporal alignment\n   279→  - Temporal binning\n   280→- Read the reference paper to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\n   281→\n   282→**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 3\".\n   283→\n   284→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   285→\n   286→---\n   287→\n   288→### Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   289→\n   290→**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \n   291→\n   292→**Actions**:\n   293→- Compare your understanding of:\n   294→  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\n   295→  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\n   296→  - Analysis described in the reference texts\n   297→- Note and investigate any discrepancies\n   298→- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\n   299→  - Experimental and behavioral variables\n   300→  - Data processing\n   301→  - Data curation\n   302→- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n   303→\n   304→**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n   305→\n   306→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   307→\n   308→---\n   309→\n   310→### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   311→\n   312→**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n   313→\n   314→**Actions**:\n   315→- Map source data elements to target format components:\n   316→  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n   317→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   318→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   319→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   320→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   321→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   322→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \n   323→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   324→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   325→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   326→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   327→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   328→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   329→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   330→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   331→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   332→\n   333→**Done when**:\n   334→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   335→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   336→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   337→\n   338→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   339→\n   340→---\n   341→\n   342→### Step 6: Script Development\n   343→\n   344→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\n   345→\n   346→**Actions**:\n   347→- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\n   348→- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   349→- Include options:\n   350→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   351→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   352→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   353→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   354→- Write efficient code:\n   355→  - Vectorize loops\n   356→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   357→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   358→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   359→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   360→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   361→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   362→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   363→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   364→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   365→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   366→\n   367→**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   368→\n   369→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   370→\n   371→---\n   372→\n   373→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   374→\n   375→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   376→\n   377→**Actions**:\n   378→1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   379→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   380→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   381→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   382→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   383→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   384→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   385→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   386→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   387→3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   388→4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   389→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   390→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   391→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   392→\n   393→**Done when**: \n   394→- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   395→- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   396→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   397→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   398→\n   399→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   400→\n   401→---\n   402→\n   403→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   404→\n   405→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   406→\n   407→**Actions**:\n   408→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   409→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   410→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   411→\n   412→**Investigate any issues**:\n   413→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   414→\n   415→**Done when**: \n   416→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   417→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   418→- Accuracies are above chance\n   419→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   420→\n   421→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   422→\n   423→---\n   424→\n   425→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   426→\n   427→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   428→\n   429→**Actions**:\n   430→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   431→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   432→3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n   433→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   434→5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n   435→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   436→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   437→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   438→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   439→\n   440→**Done when**:\n   441→- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   442→- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   443→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   444→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   445→\n   446→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   447→\n   448→---\n   449→\n   450→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   451→\n   452→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   453→\n   454→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   455→Read through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   456→\n   457→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   458→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   459→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   460→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   461→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   462→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   463→- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   464→\n   465→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   466→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   467→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   468→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   469→- Fix any mismatches. \n   470→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   471→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   472→\n   473→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   474→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \n   475→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   476→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   477→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   478→\n   479→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   480→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   481→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   482→\n   483→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   484→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   485→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   486→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   487→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   488→\n   489→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   490→\n   491→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   492→\n   493→---\n   494→\n   495→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   496→\n   497→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   498→\n   499→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   500→\n   501→**Actions**:\n   502→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   503→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   504→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   505→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n   506→\n   507→**Done when**: \n   508→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   509→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   510→\n   511→---\n   512→\n   513→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   514→\n   515→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   516→\n   517→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   518→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   519→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   520→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   521→\n   522→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n   523→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n   524→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers's accuracy for each variable\n   525→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   526→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   527→\n   528→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   529→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   530→\n   531→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   532→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   533→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   534→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   535→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   536→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n   537→\n   538→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   539→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   540→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   541→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   542→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   543→\n   544→**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   545→\n   546→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   547→\n   548→---\n   549→\n   550→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   551→\n   552→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   553→\n   554→**Actions**:\n   555→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   556→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   557→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   558→  - Validation results and tables\n   559→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   560→- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n   561→  - Dataset description\n   562→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   563→  - Output format specification\n   564→  - Key statistics\n   565→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n   566→- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   567→\n   568→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   569→\n   570→---\n   571→\n   572→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   573→\n   574→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Copy this template exactly:**\n   575→\n   576→```markdown\n   577→# Dataset Conversion Notes\n   578→\n   579→## Overview\n   580→- **Dataset**: [Name and source]\n   581→- **Date started**: [Date]\n Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference papers and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **papers**:\n  - \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\" describes how the data were collected and is in the file `datapaper.pdf`.\n  - \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\" describes how the code were used and is in the file `methodpaper.pdf`.\n  - A white paper about the data architecture is in `dataarchitecture.pdf\n- Some parts of the papers that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the methods paper are in the directory `code`.\n- **Data** from the data paper are in the directory `data`. Some data may be missing and may need to be downloaded. \n- **Documentation** about how to access the data can be found at https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/public_docs/public_introduction.html.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally align based on stimulus onset\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time since stimulus onset, continuous, time-varying\n- Trial number in block, continuous, per-trial\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Choice, binary, per-trial, left = 0, right = 1\n- Prior probability of left, per-trial, 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2\n- Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n- Whisker motion energy discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. Neural data are spike times and spike-sorted cluster metadata; there is no delta-F/F computation anywhere in the pipeline.\n    52→- The main reference preprocessing entry point is `code/code_zhang2025/src/0_data_caching.py`.\n    53→- Default preprocessing parameters in that script are:\n    54→  - `wheel-speed` as `abs(wheel velocity)`\n    66→  - `whisker-motion-energy` from left camera whisker energy, falling back to right camera if left is unavailable\n    67→  - `pupil-diameter` exists in code but is commented out in `0_data_caching.py` because some sessions lack pupil traces\n    68→- Trial curation in `prepare_data` is driven by `load_trials_and_mask(one=one, eid=eid, max_trial_len=10.0)` with defaults:\n    69→  - minimum reaction time `0.08 s`\n    70→  - maximum reaction time `2.0 s`\n    71→  - exclude trials with NaNs in `stimOn_times`, `choice`, `feedback_times`, `probabilityLeft`, `firstMovement_times`, `feedbackType`\n    72→  - exclude no-choice trials (`choice == 0`)\n    73→  - do not exclude the initial unbiased block unless explicitly requested\n    74→  - exclude trials longer than `10.0 s` from `goCue_times` to `feedback_times`\n    75→- Cluster quality:\n    76→  - `load_spiking_data` supports QC filtering through a `qc` argument, but `prepare_data` does not pass a threshold, so all loaded clusters are kept in the reference caching step.\n    77→  - Good-cluster labels are still stored in metadata as `clusters['label'] >= 1`.\n    78→- Spike binning details:\n    79→  - spikes are merged across probes before binning\n    80→  - per-trial intervals are computed from `stimOn_times + (-0.5, 1.5)`\n    81→  - multi-bin spike counts are produced with 20 ms bins\n    82→  - per-trial spike matrices are transposed to `(n_bins, n_clusters)` in the cached dataset\n    83→- Behavior alignment details:\n    84→  - continuous signals are segmented into the same per-trial `stimOn` window\n    85→  - behavior traces are linearly interpolated onto `n_bins = ceil(interval_len / binsize)` samples\n    86→  - valid intervals require data to start and end within one bin of the requested interval boundaries\n    87→- Final trial alignment is enforced by `align_spike_behavior`, which deletes any trial rejected by the trial mask or missing aligned behavior.\n    88→- Decoder scripts (`1_decode_single_session.py`, `2_decode_multi_session.py`, `3_decode_multi_region.py`) operate on the cached aligned trials and target one behavior at a time. Expected Statistics (from papers/methods)\n   145→| Statistic | Value | Source Quote |\n   146→|-----------|-------|--------------|\n   147→| Neurons (total) | 621,733 units in the public release | \"After applying these criteria, a total of 459 sessions, 699 insertions and 621,733 neurons remained, constituting the publicly released dataset.\" |\n   148→| Neurons / session | ~1,355 units/session in public release (inferred from 621,733 / 459) | Same quote as above; per-session value is an inference from reported totals. |\n   149→| Subjects | 139 mice | \"We trained 139 mice (94 male and 45 female) on the International Brain Laboratory (IBL) decision-making task...\" |\n   150→| Sessions / subject | ~3.30 sessions/subject in public release (inferred from 459 / 139) | Derived from the reported 459 sessions and 139 mice. |\n   151→| Trials (total) | Not explicitly reported; lower bound >= 183,600 in the analysis set | \"Only sessions with at least 400 trials were retained for further analyses.\" |\n   152→| Trials / session | >= 400 for analyses; >= 250 for release inclusion | \"Only sessions with at least 400 trials were retained for further analyses.\" and \"Sessions were included in the data release if the mice performed at least 250 trials...\" |\n   153→| Neural data time bin | 20 ms in the reference code and for dynamic behaviors in the methods paper; methods paper states 50 ms bins for choice/prior trial-aligned analyses | \"Recordings are split into 2-s trials, each divided into 20-ms bins...\" and \"Within each trial, we segment neural activity into 50-ms non-overlapping time bins.\" |\n   154→| Behavior data time bin | Wheel/whisker decoded in 20 ms bins; raw behavior sampled at camera/wheel rates | \"Wheel values ... were averaged in nonoverlapping 20-ms bins...\" and \"Choice and prior are static within a trial, while wheel speed and whisker motion energy are time-varying signals sampled at 60 Hz.\" |\n   155→| Reward rate | Not explicitly reported in provided texts | Not directly stated in `methods.txt`. |\n   156→| Initial unbiased block length | 90 trials | \"For the first 90 trials, the stimulus appears randomly on either side with equal probability...\" |\n   157→| Prior probability levels | 0.2, 0.5, 0.8 block structure | \"...for the first 90 trials... equal probability\" and \"in right-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 80% of the trials, whereas in left-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 20% of the trials.\" |\n   158→| Mean biased block length | 51 trials (empirical mean) | \"Blocks lasted for between 20 and 100 trials, which were drawn from a truncated geometric distribution (empirical mean of 51 trials).\" |\n   159→| Well-isolated neurons | 75,708 | \"Out of the 621,733 units collected, 75,708 were considered well-isolated neurons.\" |\n   160→| Sessions used in methods paper decoding | 433 sessions, 270 brain regions | \"We apply our models to 433 IBL sessions, covering 270 brain regions and four behavioral variables...\" |\n   161→\n   162→\n   163→### Processing Details\n   164→- The task is the IBL visual decision-making paradigm with wheel-turn choice, 90 unbiased trials at session start, then alternating biased blocks.\n   165→- The methods paper describes two related preprocessing setups:\n   166→  - trial-aligned data for `choice` and `prior`\n   167→  - first-movement aligned or trial-unaligned data for dynamic behaviors (`wheel speed`, `whisker motion energy`)\n   168→- Temporal alignment from the methods paper:\n   169→  - `choice`: align to stimulus onset, use neural activity from `-0.5 s` to `+1.5 s`\n   170→  - `prior`: align to stimulus onset, use a pre-stimulus window from `-0.6 s` to `-0.1 s`\n   171→  - `wheel speed` and `whisker motion energy`: align to first movement onset, decode from movement onset to `+1.0 s`; the datapaper decoding methods additionally mention wheel values averaged from `-0.2 s` to `+1.0 s` around first movement\n   172→- Binning from the methods paper:\n   173→  - 20 ms bins are the default in the method paper overview and in the provided reference code\n   174→  - 50 ms bins are explicitly stated for choice/prior in the copied methods text\n   175→  - this is a paper-vs-code discrepancy that must be resolved in Step 4\n   176→- Whisker motion energy is defined as the mean absolute difference between adjacent video frames in a whisker-pad bounding box anchored between nose tip and eye.\n   177→- Sessions in the datapaper release passed behavioral and hardware QC; analyses further retained sessions with at least 400 trials.\n   178→- The datapaper decoding analyses combined neurons across probes within a session rather than decoding each probe independently.\n   179→\n   180→### Curation Steps\n   181→\n   182→**Neuron curation rules**:\n   183→- Datapaper analyses exclude neurons that fail the well-isolated single-unit criteria described in the paper.\n   184→- Explicit text in `methods.txt` states the criteria include amplitude, noise cut-off, and refractory-period-violation checks.\n   185→- Final analyses in the datapaper also restrict to grey-matter regions with at least five well-isolated neurons per session and at least two sessions per region.\n   186→\n   187→**Trial curation rules**:\n   188→- Exclude trials if any of these events are missing: `choice`, `probabilityLeft`, `feedbackType`, `feedback times`, `stimOn times`, `firstMovement times`.\n   189→- Exclude trials with stimulus-onset to first-movement reaction time outside `0.08 s` to `2.00 s`.\n   190→\n   191→### Decoders Trained\n   192→| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n   193→| Choice | Methods paper figure text reports example-session AUC improvement from about `0.66` (single-session) to `0.72` (multi-session), with oracle around `0.79`. |\n   194→| Prior | Methods paper figure text reports example-session correlation improvement from about `0.05` (single-session) to `0.34` (multi-session), with oracle around `0.65`. |\n   195→| Wheel speed | Methods paper states multi-session models outperform single-session baselines; figure examples on page 5 show clearly higher correlation-like summary values for multi-session RRR than ridge, but not a single cohort-average number in the extracted text. |\n   196→| Whisker motion energy | Methods paper states multi-session models outperform single-session baselines; page 5 example traces/summary values indicate substantial improvement over ridge but the extracted text does not provide a single cohort-average number. |\n   197→\n   198→---\n   199→\n   200→## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   201→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   202→\n   203→### Discrepancies Found\n   204→| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Papers say | Resolution |\n   205→|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n   206→| Session roster size | `code/code_zhang2025/data/bwm_release.csv` contains 459 sessions (699 probe insertions, 139 mice). | Local `Brainwidemap` cache table contains 480 sessions because it includes a newer release; the 459 code-release sessions are all present locally. | Datapaper reports 459 public-release sessions; methods paper reports 433 sessions used for decoding. | Treat the 459-session `bwm_release.csv` used by the reference code as the canonical release roster for this workspace. Ignore the extra 21 sessions from the newer 480-session cache. Investigate the 459-to-433 reduction later as an analysis-specific subset, not as the base dataset definition. |\n   207→| Sessions with all required modalities | Reference code assumes wheel and whisker behaviors can be loaded; it does not precompute a task-specific session count. | Among the 459 code-release sessions present locally, 439 have spikes, trials, wheel, and whisker motion energy on disk. | Methods paper uses 433 sessions for its IBL decoding analyses. | Use 439 as the starting locally complete set for this task. The remaining 6-session gap to the paper’s 433 likely reflects additional analysis-time failures or exclusions beyond simple file presence; verify this later during conversion/validation. |\n   208→| Neuron QC | `prepare_data()` loads all spike-sorted clusters; it stores `good_clusters = label >= 1` in metadata but does not filter them out before binning. | Raw local sessions contain many more clusters than well-isolated units (for the 439 complete code-release sessions: 594k+ raw clusters vs far fewer good-label units). | Datapaper analyses report 75,708 well-isolated neurons and explicitly describe neuron QC. | Use the code’s stored QC label to filter to `label >= 1` clusters during conversion. This preserves the paper’s curation intent while still relying on the provided code path and metadata definitions. |\n   209→| Region handling | Reference caching code pools all Beryl regions together (`single_region=False`) and only subsets by region later in decoder scripts. | Local cluster metadata include per-cluster brain-region labels. | Datapaper analyses restrict to grey-matter regions with >=5 well-isolated neurons/session and >=2 sessions/region; methods paper decodes specific regions separately in some figures. | For the requested converted dataset, keep per-neuron region labels for all neurons and do not collapse sessions by region. This preserves downstream flexibility and matches the target format. |\n   210→| Temporal alignment and binning | Reference code uses one common setup for cached trials: `stimOn_times`, window `[-0.5, 1.5]`, binsize `0.02 s`, all behaviors aligned into the same 2 s trial grid. | Local files support stimulus-onset times, first-movement times, wheel traces, and whisker motion-energy traces. | Methods paper describes mixed target-specific preprocessing: choice aligned to stimulus onset; prior pre-stimulus; wheel/whisker aligned to first movement; 50 ms bins for choice/prior and 20 ms bins for dynamic behaviors. | Because the user explicitly requires \"Temporally align based on stimulus onset\" and a single common dataset with simultaneous outputs, prioritize the executable reference code’s common `stimOn`-aligned 2 s / 20 ms representation, while documenting that this is the main paper-vs-task-driven deviation. |\n   211→| Trial filtering | Reference code excludes missing-event trials, RT outside 0.08–2.0 s, no-choice trials, and trials longer than 10 s. | Raw trial tables contain the needed event columns to reproduce these masks. | Papers explicitly mention missing-event and 0.08–2.0 s RT exclusions; they do not mention the code’s `max_trial_len=10.0` detail in the copied text. | Reproduce the reference code trial mask exactly, including `max_trial_len=10.0`, because it is the executable implementation provided for this project. |\n   212→| Dynamic behavior representation | Reference code keeps wheel speed and whisker motion energy continuous and interpolated per time bin. | Raw wheel and whisker traces are continuous float signals at wheel/camera sampling rates. | Papers decode these as continuous variables with regression. | The user task explicitly requires categorical outputs, so discretize these continuous traces into 3 bins only after reproducing the reference loading/alignment. This is an allowed task-driven deviation, not a loading mismatch. |\n   213→\n   214→Final understanding after reconciling sources:\n   215→- Base session roster should come from the 459-session release used by the reference code, not the newer 480-session cache metadata.\n   216→- The directly usable local subset for the requested four-output decoder is 439 sessions with complete required modalities.\n   217→- The provided executable code is the strongest source for exact loading/alignment behavior in this workspace.\n   218→- The papers remain the strongest source for scientific curation intent and expected global dataset statistics.\n   219→- The main implementation choice is therefore: use reference-code loading/alignment and paper-informed sanity checks, while applying only the task-required deviations (stimulus-onset alignment for all requested variables and discretization of continuous outputs).\n   220→\n   221→---\n   222→\n   223→## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   224→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   225→\n   226→### Variable Mapping\n   227→| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n   228→|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n   229→| `spikes.times`, `spikes.clusters`, probe cluster metadata | `neural` | Merge probes per session, keep well-isolated clusters with `label >= 1`, apply trial mask, bin spike counts in `stimOn_times + [-0.5, 1.5]` using `0.02 s` bins, store per trial as `(n_neurons, 100)` after transposing from reference `(100, n_neurons)` | `load_spiking_data(qc=1)`, `merge_probes`, `bin_spiking_data` | This follows the paper’s neuron curation while keeping the reference loading/binding machinery. |\n   230→| Derived common bin time axis relative to `stimOn_times` | `input[0]` | One continuous time channel repeated for every trial, length 100; use the same 20 ms trial grid as neural/activity outputs | derived from `time_window`, `binsize` used by `bin_spiking_data` / `get_behavior_per_interval` | User-required decoder input; not present in reference code, so derive directly from the adopted alignment grid. |\n   231→| `trials.probabilityLeft` block structure | `input[1]` | Compute trial number within current block on the original trial order, then subset to retained trials and repeat across time bins | `load_trials_and_mask` for retained-trial order | Reset counter whenever `probabilityLeft` changes; preserve original experimental indexing rather than recomputing after exclusions. |\n   232→| `trials.choice` | `output[0]` | Map raw IBL choice to task labels and repeat across time bins: raw `+1 -> left -> 0`, raw `-1 -> right -> 1`; no-go (`0`) excluded by trial mask | `load_trials_and_mask`, `bin_behaviors` | Official IBL docs indicate raw choice encodes wheel direction; for the requested dataset convert to left/right categorical choice. |\n   233→| `trials.probabilityLeft` | `output[1]` | Map `0.2 -> 0`, `0.5 -> 1`, `0.8 -> 2` and repeat across time bins | `bin_behaviors` | This is the requested discrete prior output. |\n   234→| Wheel trace loaded via `SessionLoader` / reference behavior loader | `output[2]` | Load wheel speed as `abs(velocity)`, align to stimulus onset on the same 2 s / 20 ms grid as neural data, then discretize continuous values into 3 global bins | `load_target_behavior('wheel-speed')`, `get_behavior_per_interval`, `bin_behaviors` | This is a task-driven deviation from the paper’s first-movement alignment, required because the user explicitly requests stimulus-onset alignment for the full dataset. |\n   235→| Whisker motion-energy trace loaded via `SessionLoader` / reference behavior loader | `output[3]` | Load left whisker motion energy when available, otherwise right; align to stimulus onset on the same 2 s / 20 ms grid, then discretize into 3 global bins | `load_target_behavior('left/right-whisker-motion-energy')`, `get_behavior_per_interval`, `bin_behaviors` | Same alignment/deviation rationale as wheel speed. |\n   236→| `subject` from `bwm_release.csv` and session path metadata | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Build unique subject list and index sessions in converted order | `code/code_zhang2025/data/bwm_release.csv` | Session order will be fixed and deterministic in the conversion script. |\n   237→| `clusters['acronym']` / stored `cluster_regions` | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | Build global vocabulary of original cluster acronyms and map each neuron to its region index | `prepare_data` metadata output | Use original acronyms rather than Beryl-collapse in the final dataset to match what the reference code stores. |\n   238→\n   239→### Key Decisions\n   240→1. **Base session roster**: Start from the 459-session `bwm_release.csv` used by the reference code, then require local availability of spikes, trials, wheel, and whisker motion energy for the requested joint decoder task.\n   241→2. **Common alignment grid**: Use one common `stimOn_times`-aligned `[-0.5, 1.5]` window with 20 ms bins for neural, inputs, and outputs because that matches the executable reference code and the user explicitly requires stimulus-onset alignment.\n   242→3. **Common tensor shape**: Represent all inputs and outputs as time-varying arrays with shape `(n_channels, 100)` per trial, repeating per-trial variables across the 100 bins. This keeps the converted dataset uniform and decoder-friendly.\n   243→4. **Choice remapping**: Convert raw IBL `choice` values to left/right semantic labels required by the task: raw `+1` becomes left (`0`), raw `-1` becomes right (`1`).\n   244→5. **Prior representation**: Use `probabilityLeft` directly as the prior variable and encode its three observed task values as categorical labels `0/1/2` for `0.2/0.5/0.8`.\n   245→6. **Trial number in block**: Derive this from the original experimental trial order before dropping invalid trials, then subset. This preserves the true block progression.\n   246→7. **Continuous-output discretization**: Compute global tertile thresholds from all retained aligned wheel-speed samples and all retained aligned whisker-motion-energy samples, respectively, then apply those thresholds consistently across all sessions.\n   247→8. **Neuron filtering strategy**: Use well-isolated clusters only (`label >= 1`) during conversion, because this is the closest executable approximation to the paper’s stated neuron curation and keeps total dataset size aligned with the reported 75,708-neuron scale.\n   248→9. **Whisker camera fallback**: Use left whisker motion energy when available and fall back to right whisker motion energy otherwise, matching the provided code.\n   249→\n   250→### Planned Sanity Checks\n   251→- [ ] Neural spot-check: for at least 3 session/trial examples, compare converted spike matrices against direct calls to the reference binning logic (`bin_spiking_data`) with `np.allclose()`.\n   252→- [ ] Behavior alignment spot-check: for at least 3 session/trial examples, compare continuous aligned wheel-speed and whisker traces before discretization against direct reference interpolation (`get_behavior_per_interval` / `bin_behaviors`) with `np.allclose()`.\n   253→- [ ] Trial-variable spot-check: verify `choice`, `probabilityLeft`, and derived `trial_number_in_block` against the raw trial table on at least 3 specific trials per checked session.\n   254→- [ ] Session-count sanity check: confirm converted session count equals the number of release sessions that have all required local modalities and survive downstream validity checks.\n   255→- [ ] Distribution sanity check: verify prior class distribution reflects the expected `0.5` first block followed by `0.2/0.8` biased blocks; wheel and whisker discretization should not collapse nearly all samples into one class.\n   256→- [ ] Metadata sanity check: confirm `time_bin_size = 20 ms`, `off_start = -0.5 s`, `off_end = 1.5 s`, and `temporal_alignment_event = stimulus onset` for every converted session.\n   257→\n   258→---\n   259→\n   260→## Step 6: Script Development\n optional QC threshold exists but is not used by `prepare_data`. |\n| `merge_probes` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | PROCESSING | Merges spikes and clusters across probes in a session, reindexes clusters, and sorts merged spikes by time. |\n| `load_trials_and_mask` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | CURATION | Loads session trials and creates a trial inclusion mask based on reaction time, trial duration, NaNs in required events, and no-choice trials. |\n| `prepare_data` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | LOADING | Main session loader used by the paper code: merges probes, loads trials/mask, loads continuous behaviors, and packages metadata. |\n| `list_brain_regions` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | PROCESSING | Maps cluster acronyms to Beryl regions and lists available regions. |\n| `select_brain_regions` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | CURATION | Selects cluster ids for requested Beryl region(s); in the caching script this is all regions together. |\n| `get_spike_data_per_interval` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | PROCESSING | Bins spikes into trial-aligned intervals with multiprocessing. |\n| `bin_spiking_data` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | PROCESSING | Creates per-trial spike-count matrices aligned to `align_time`; output shape per trial is `(n_bins, n_clusters)`. |\n| `load_target_behavior` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | LOADING | Loads continuous behavior streams such as wheel velocity/speed and whisker motion energy using `SessionLoader` or camera features. |\n| `get_behavior_per_interval` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | PROCESSING | Segments continuous behavior into trial windows and linearly interpolates it to aligned bins. |\n| `bin_behaviors` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | PROCESSING | Builds per-trial behavior arrays and trial-level variables (`choice`, `block`, `reward`, `contrast`). |\n| `align_spike_behavior` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` | CURATION | Drops trials where neural/behavior data are missing or rejected by the trial mask; reshapes behavior arrays to one row per retained trial. |\n| `create_dataset` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/dataset_utils.py` | PROCESSING | Converts per-trial spike matrices to CSR sparse storage and packages behavior plus session metadata in a Hugging Face dataset. |\n| `standardize_spike_data` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/data_loader_utils.py` | PROCESSING | Z-scores spike counts across trials separately for each time bin and neuron before decoding. |\n| `SingleSessionDataset` | `code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/data_loader_utils.py` | PROCESSING | Loads cached per-session arrays, optionally subsets by region, standardizes spikes, and one-hot encodes classification targets. |\n| `0_data_caching.py` main loop | `code/code_zhang2025/src/0_data_caching.py` | PROCESSING | Reference preprocessing pipeline used to cache aligned datasets for decoding. |\n\n### Notes\n- Reference code is for electrophysiology, not imaging. Neural data are spike times and spike-sorted cluster metadata; there is no delta-F/F computation anywhere in the pipeline.\n- The main reference preprocessing entry point is `code/code_zhang2025/src/0_data_caching.py`.\n- Default preprocessing parameters in that script are:\n  - `align_time='stimOn_times'`\n  - `time_window=(-0.5, 1.5)` seconds\n  - `interval_len=2`\n  - `binsize=0.02` seconds\n  - `single_region=False` so all available Beryl regions are pooled unless a decoder later subsets them.\n- Trial-level variables created in the reference code are:\n  - `choice` from `trials_df['choice']`\n  - `block` from `trials_df['probabilityLeft']`\n  - `reward` from `(rewardVolume > 1).astype(int)`\n  - `contrast` from signed contrast difference computed from left/right contrasts\n- Continuous behaviors used in the reference code are:\n  - `wheel-speed` as `abs(wheel velocity)`\n  - `whisker-motion-energy` from left camera whisker energy, falling back to right camera if left is unavailable\n  - `pupil-diameter` exists in code but is commented out in `0_data_caching.py` because some sessions lack pupil traces\n- Trial curation in `prepare_data` is driven by `load_trials_and_mask(one=one, eid=eid, max_trial_len=10.0)` with defaults:\n  - minimum reaction time `0.08 s`\n  - maximum reaction time `2.0 s`\n  - exclude trials with NaNs in `stimOn_times`, `choice`, `feedback_times`, `probabilityLeft`, `firstMovement_times`, `feedbackType`\n  - exclude no-choice trials (`choice == 0`)\n  - do not exclude the initial unbiased block unless explicitly requested\n  - exclude trials longer than `10.0 s` from `goCue_times` to `feedback_times`\n- Cluster quality:\n  - `load_spiking_data` supports QC filtering through a `qc` argument, but `prepare_data` does not pass a threshold, so all loaded clusters are kept in the reference caching step.\n  - Good-cluster labels are still stored in metadata as `clusters['label'] >= 1`.\n- Spike binning details:\n  - spikes are merged across probes before binning\n  - per-trial intervals are computed from `stimOn_times + (-0.5, 1.5)`\n  - multi-bin spike counts are produced with 20 ms bins\n  - per-trial spike matrices are transposed to `(n_bins, n_clusters)` in the cached dataset\n- Behavior alignment details:\n  - continuous signals are segmented into the same per-trial `stimOn` window\n  - behavior traces are linearly interpolated onto `n_bins = ceil(interval_len / binsize)` samples\n  - valid intervals require data to start and end within one bin of the requested interval boundaries\n- Final trial alignment is enforced by `align_spike_behavior`, which deletes any trial rejected by the trial mask or missing aligned behavior.\n- Decoder scripts (`1_decode_single_session.py`, `2_decode_multi_session.py`, `3_decode_multi_region.py`) operate on the cached aligned trials and target one behavior at a time. Classification is used for `choice`; regression is used for continuous behaviors in the reference code.\n\n---\n\n## Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Data Structure\n- Data are stored in `data/one_cache`, using the IBL ONE cache layout rather than a custom project format.\n- Release-level metadata tables are present in:\n  -
